var(--grid-column-gap));}}</style><div class="m_96bdd299 mantine-Grid-col __m__-_R_2muqssm_"><article class="blog-article-body"><address class="article-author visually-hidden">By <span><a rel="author" href="/blog/authors/luigino-camastra">Luigino Camastra</a></span><span>, <a rel="author" href="/blog/authors/igor-morgenstern">Igor Morgenstern</a></span><span>, <a rel="author" href="/blog/authors/jan-vojtesek">Jan Vojtěšek</a></span></address><div style="--container-size:var(--container-size-xxl);max-width:calc(97.5rem * var(--mantine-scale));padding-inline:0rem" class="m_7485cace mantine-Container-root" data-size="xxl" data-strategy="block" id="post" data-component="post-content"><div class="RichText_wrapper__xZRh7"><p><strong>Introduction</strong></p><p>We discovered an installer downloaded from the official website of <a href="http://monpass.mn/"><code>MonPass</code></a>, a major certification authority (CA) in Mongolia in East Asia that was backdoored with Cobalt Strike binaries. We immediately notified MonPass on 22 April 2021 of our findings and encouraged them to address their compromised server and notify those who downloaded the backdoored client.</p><p>We have confirmed with MonPass that they have taken steps to address these issues and are now presenting our analysis.</p><p>Our analysis beginning in April 2021 indicates that a public web server hosted by MonPass was breached potentially eight separate times: we found eight different webshells and backdoors on this server. We also found that the MonPass client available for download from 8 February 2021 until 3 March 2021 was backdoored.&nbsp;</p><p>This research provides analysis of relevant backdoored installers and other samples that we found occurring in the wild. Also during our investigation we observed relevant <a href="https://hello.global.ntt/-/media/ntt/global/insights/white-papers/the-operations-of-winnti-group.pdf">research from NTT Ltd</a> so some technical details or IoCs may overlap.</p><p>All the samples are highly similar and share the same pdb path:</p><p><code>C:\Users\test\Desktop\fishmaster\x64\Release\fishmaster.pdb</code> and the string: <code>Bidenhappyhappyhappy</code>.</p></div><div style="padding-inline:0rem;margin-block:var(--mantine-spacing-md)" class="imageWithCaption_container__fP6ro imageWithCaption_fullWidth__4Ncwy m_7485cace mantine-Container-root" data-strategy="block"><picture id="the-picture"><source srcSet="https://www.gendigital.com/blog/sites/default/files/styles/blogs_paragraph_image_large/public/2024-06/pasted%20image%200_19.png.webp?itok=73Vy-6zv 1x, https://www.gendigital.com/blog/sites/default/files/styles/blogs_paragraph_image_large_2x/public/2024-06/pasted%20image%200_19.png.webp?itok=YFzTrIXp 2x" media="(min-width: 1020px)"/><source https://www.gendigital.com/blog/sites/default/files/styles/blogs_paragraph_image_medium_2x/public/2024-06/pasted%20image%200_19.png.webp?itok=IUJ0f_UT 2x" media="(min-width: 720px)"/><source srcSet="https://www.gendigital.com/blog/sites/default/files/styles/blogs_paragraph_image_small/public/2024-06/pasted%20image%200_19.png.webp?itok=Fc2Cd5n0 1x, https://www.gendigital.com/blog/sites/default/files/styles/blogs_paragraph_image_small_2x/public/2024-06/pasted%20image%200_19.png.webp?itok=y19zMEdL 2x" media="(min-width: 420px)"/><img alt="Figure 1: Pdb path and specific string" width="300" height="300" decoding="async" data-nimg="1" class="imageWithCaption_image__xW70R" style="color:transparent;width:100%;height:100%" class="mantine-focus-auto bodyText imageWithCaption_caption__hCQ67 picture-caption m_b6d8b162 mantine-Text-root">Figure 1: Pdb path and specific string</p></div><div class="RichText_wrapper__xZRh7"><p><strong>Technical details</strong></p><p>The malicious installer is an unsigned PE file. It starts by downloading the legitimate version of the installer from the MonPass official website. This legitimate version is dropped to the <code>C:\Users\Public\</code> folder and executed under a new process. This guarantees that the installer behaves as expected, meaning that a regular user is unlikely to notice anything suspicious.</p><p>Additional similar installers were also found in the wild, with SHA256 hashes:&nbsp; <code>e2596f015378234d9308549f08bcdca8eadbf69e488355cddc9c2425f77b7535 </code>and&nbsp;<code>f21a9c69bfca6f0633ba1e669e5cf86bd8fc55b2529cd9</code></p></div><div style="padding-inline:0rem;margin-block:var(--mantine-spacing-md)" class="imageWithCaption_container__fP6ro imageWithCaption_fullWidth__4Ncwy m_7485cace mantine-Container-root" data-strategy="block"><picture id="the-picture"><source https://www.gendigital.com/blog/sites/default/files/styles/blogs_paragraph_image_large_2x/public/2024-06/pasted%20image%200-1_8.png.webp?itok=vSoYqWvj 2x" media="(min-width: 1020px)"/><source 2: This image is not as innocent as it may seem." width="300" height="300" decoding="async" data-nimg="1" class="imageWithCaption_image__xW70R" style="color:transparent;width:100%;height:100%" 2x" src="/blog/_next/image?url=https%3A%2F%2Fwww.gendigital.com%2Fblog%2Fsites%2Fdefault%2Ffiles%2Fstyles%2Fblogs_paragraph_image_small%2Fpublic%2F2024-06%2Fpasted%2520image%25200-1_8.png.webp%3Fitok%3Dt5FtEeGu&amp;w=640&amp;q=75"/></picture><p class="mantine-focus-auto bodyText imageWithCaption_caption__hCQ67 picture-caption m_b6d8b162 mantine-Text-root">Figure 2: This image is not as innocent as it may seem.</p></div><div class="RichText_wrapper__xZRh7"><p>The attackers decided to use steganography to transfer shellcode to their victims. On execution, the malware downloads a bitmap image file from http://download.google-images[.]ml:8880/download/37.bmp as shown in <code>figure 2</code>.&nbsp;</p><p>The download is performed slightly unusually in two HTTP requests. The first request uses the HEAD method to retrieve the Content-Length, followed by a second GET request to actually download the image. After the picture is downloaded, the malware extracts the encrypted payload as follows. The hidden data is expected to be up to 0x76C bytes. Starting with the 3rd byte in image data it copies each 4th byte. The resulting data represents an ASCII string of hexadecimal characters which is later decoded into their respective binary values. These bytes are then XOR decrypted using the hardcoded key <code>miat_mg</code>, resulting in a Cobalt-Strike beacon.</p><p>We have seen multiple versions of this backdoored installer, each with slightly modified decryptors.&nbsp;</p><p>In version (<code>f21a9c69bfca6f0633ba1e669e5cf86bd8fc55b2529cd9b064ff9e2e129525e8</code>) the XOR decryption was stripped.<br><br>In the version (<code>e2596f015378234d9308549f08bcdca8eadbf69e488355cddc9c2425f77b7535</code>) basic anti-analysis tricks were stripped. In Figure 3, you can see different time stamps and the same rich headers.</p></div><div style="padding-inline:0rem;margin-block:var(--mantine-spacing-md)" class="imageWithCaption_container__fP6ro class="mantine-focus-auto bodyText imageWithCaption_caption__hCQ67 picture-caption m_b6d8b162 mantine-Text-root">Figure 4: Rich header.</p></div><div class="RichText_wrapper__xZRh7"><p>In the backdoored installer we also observed some basic anti-analysis techniques used in an attempt to avoid detection. In particular, we observed checks for the number of processors using the <code><em>GetSystemInfo</em></code> function, the amount of physical memory using the <code><em>GlobalMemoryStatusEx</em></code> function and the disk capacity using the <code>IOCTL_DISK_GET_DRIVE_GEOMETRY</code> IOCTL call. If any of the obtained values are suspiciously low, the malware terminates immediately.</p></div><div style="padding-inline:0rem;margin-block:var(--mantine-spacing-md)" class="mantine-focus-auto bodyText imageWithCaption_caption__hCQ67 picture-caption m_b6d8b162 mantine-Text-root">Figure 8: VT hit</p></div><div class="RichText_wrapper__xZRh7"><p>We analyzed the sample and found out that the sample was very similar to infected installers found in our customers. The sample contained anti-analysis techniques using the same XOR decryption and also contained similar C2 server addresses (<code>hxxp://download.google-images.ml:8880/download/x37.bmp</code>) as observed in previous backdoored installers. The sample also contained references to the link (<code>hxxps://webplus-cn-hongkong-s-5faf81e0d937f14c9ddbe5a0.oss-cn-hongkong.aliyuncs[.]com/Silverlight_ins.exe</code>) and the file path <code>C:\users\public\Silverlight_ins.exe</code>; however these did not appear to be in use. The sample name is also unusual –&nbsp; <code>Browser_plugin (8).exe</code> – we speculate that this may be a test sample uploaded by the actor.&nbsp;</p><p>In VT we saw another hash (<code>4a43fa8a3305c2a17f6a383fb68f02515f589ba112c6e95f570ce421cc690910</code>) again with the name <code>Browser_plugin.exe</code>. According to VT this sample has been downloaded from <code>hxxps://jquery-code.ml/Download/Browser_Plugin.exe</code>. It was downloading a PDF from <code>hxxp://37.61.205.212:8880/dow/Aili.pdf</code> PDF file <code>Aili.pdf.</code></p></div><div style="padding-inline:0rem;margin-block:var(--mantine-spacing-md)" class="RichText_wrapper__xZRh7"><p>Afterwards it has the similar functionalities as previously mentioned samples from VT. That means it was downloading and decrypting Cobalt strike beacon from <code>hxxp://micsoftin.us:2086/dow/83.bmp</code></p><p>In our database we again found the similar sample but with the name <code>Browser_plugin (1).exe</code>. This sample was downloaded from <code>hxxp://37.61.205.212:8880/download/Browers_plugin.exe</code>, we saw it on Feb 4, 2021. It doesn’t install any legitimate software, it just shows a MessageBox. It contains C&amp;C address (<code>hxxp://download.google-images.ml:8880/downloa/37.bmp</code>), (Note: there is a typo in the directory name: downloa).&nbsp;</p><h2><strong>Compromised Web server content</strong></h2><p>On the breached web server, where you were able to download backdoored installer we found two executables <code>DNS.exe</code> (<code>456b69628caa3edf828f4ba987223812cbe5bbf91e6bbf167e21bef25de7c9d2</code>) and again <code>Browser_plugin.exe</code> (<code>5cebdb91c7fc3abac1248deea6ed6b87fde621d0d407923de7e1365ce13d6dbe</code>).&nbsp;</p><h3><strong>DNS.exe</strong></h3><p>It downloads from (<code>hxxp://download.google-images.ml:8880/download/DNSs.bat</code>) C&amp;C server bat file, that is saved in <code>C:\users\public\DNS.bat</code>. It contains this script:</p></div><div style="padding-inline:0rem;margin-block:var(--mantine-spacing-md)" class="imageWithCaption_container__fP6ro imageWithCaption_fullWidth__4Ncwy m_7485cace mantine-Container-root" data-strategy="block"><picture id="the-picture"><source class="mantine-focus-auto bodyText imageWithCaption_caption__hCQ67 picture-caption m_b6d8b162 mantine-Text-root">Figure 10: DNS.bat script</p></div><div class="RichText_wrapper__xZRh7"><p>In the second part of the instance, it contains the similar functionality and the same address of C&amp;C server as the backdoored installer that we mentioned earlier.&nbsp;</p><h3><strong>Browser_plugin.exe</strong></h3><p>(<code>5cebdb91c7fc3abac1248deea6ed6b87fde621d0d407923de7e1365ce13d6dbe</code>)</p><p>This sample is very similar to this one (<code>4a43fa8a3305c2a17f6a383fb68f02515f589ba112c6e95f570ce421cc690910</code>) with the same address of C&amp;C server, but it doesn’t download any additional document.&nbsp;</p><h3><strong>C&amp;C server analysis</strong></h3><p>We checked the malicious web server <code>hxxps://jquery-code.ml</code>, from where (<code>4A43FA8A3305C2A17F6A383FB68F02515F589BA112C6E95F570CE421CC690910</code>) <code>Browser_plugin.exe</code> has been downloading. The malicious web server looks identical to the legitimate one <code>https://code.jquery.com/</code> the difference is the certificate. The legitimate server <code>https://code.jquery.com</code> is signed by Sectigo Limited while the malicious server is signed by Cloudflare, Inc.</p></div><div style="padding-inline:0rem;margin-block:var(--mantine-spacing-md)" class="imageWithCaption_container__fP6ro imageWithCaption_fullWidth__4Ncwy m_7485cace mantine-Container-root" class="mantine-focus-auto bodyText imageWithCaption_caption__hCQ67 picture-caption m_b6d8b162 mantine-Text-root">Figure 11: Comparing two sites</p></div><div class="RichText_wrapper__xZRh7"><p><strong>Conclusion</strong></p><p>This blog post outlines our findings regarding the MonPass client backdoored with Cobalt Strike.&nbsp;</p><p>In our research we found additional variants on VirusTotal in addition to those we found on the compromised MonPass web server.&nbsp;</p><p>In our analysis of the compromised client and variants, we’ve shown that the malware was using steganography to decrypt Cobalt Strike beacon.&nbsp;</p><p>At this time, we’re not able to make attribution of these attacks with an appropriate level of confidence. However it’s clear that the attackers clearly intended to spread malware to users in Mongolia by compromising a trustworthy source, which in this case is a CA in Mongolia.</p><p>Most importantly, anyone that has downloaded the MonPass client <code>between 8 February 2021 until 3 March 2021</code> should take steps to look for and remove the client and the backdoor it installed.&nbsp;</p><p>I would like to thank <a href="https://decoded.avast.io/author/janrubin/">Jan Rubín</a> for helping me with this research.</p><p><strong>Timeline of communication:</strong></p><ul><li data-list-item-id="e7eabad4d21f85ba9a9ed419a20998d37">March 24. 2021 – Discovered backdoored installer</li><li data-list-item-id="e8d7de5073698575bdd8d9566bf007ec6">April 8. 2021 – Initial contact with Monpass through <a href="https://mncert.org/#/en/">MN CERT/CC</a> providing findings.</li><li data-list-item-id="effb9ec355fe1fefa5623ca1fe24176f2">April 20. 2021 – MonPass shared a forensic image of an infected web server with Avast Threat Labs.</li><li data-list-item-id="eb15fae9d2778c710db010b00586f4519">April 22. 2021 – Avast provided information about the incident and findings from the forensics image in a call with MonPass and MN CERT/CC.</li><li data-list-item-id="e8105dcb7e169360a631c48acaf980c1a">May 3. 2021 – Avast followed up with MonPass in email. No response.</li><li data-list-item-id="e2db9146a0bdd66b9cf42026365680bc8">May 10. 2021 – Avast sent additional follow up email.</li><li data-list-item-id="e5d2e225d922c6c3ca99e586af1ee9597">June 4, 2021 – MonPass replied asking for information already provided on April 22, 2021.</li><li data-list-item-id="ec890ed4b283f34667a2386d1b5c798bd">June 14. 2021 – Follow up from Avast to MonPass, no response</li><li data-list-item-id="e487df344ecb24a55d12c053d6b59333d">June 29, 2021 – Final email to MonPass indicating our plans to publish with a draft of the blog for feedback.</li><li data-list-item-id="ea8e219e1a79c440f3b0f7b29d2475300">June 29, 2021 – Information from MonPass indicating they’ve resolved the issues and notified affected customers.</li><li data-list-item-id="e905ee901600e8a3bf0c0de076ba8b3bd">July 1, 2021 – Blog published.</li></ul><p><strong>Indicators of Compromise (IoC)</strong></p><ul><li victims' mailboxes independently of any stolen cookies, even after the victim changes their password and revokes all sessions.\u003c/p\u003e\n\u003c/li\u003e\n\u003cli\u003e\n\u003cp\u003eThe capability.\u003c/p\u003e\n\u003c/li\u003e\n\u003cli\u003e\n\u003cp\u003eGen Threat Labs has tracked down the module behind the feature and found that, rather than exploiting a Gmail vulnerability, it silently installs a malicious extension into the victim's Google Chrome browser.\u003c/p\u003e\n\u003c/li\u003e\n\u003cli\u003e\n\u003cp\u003eIn this blog post, we provide a technical breakdown of the malicious module and the techniques Remus uses to maintain continuous access to victims' mailboxes.\u003c/p\u003e\n\u003c/li\u003e\n\u003c/ul\u003e\n\u003ch2\u003eIntroduction\u003c/h2\u003e\n\u003cp\u003eImagine a Gmail vulnerability that allows threat actors to silently access victims' mailboxes independently of any stolen cookies, and to keep that access even after the victim changes their password and revokes all sessions. That is exactly what the developers of Remus Stealer began promoting on underground forums on November 8, 2026, boasting of being the only ones on the market offering such a feature and attributing it to a Gmail zero-day vulnerability they had supposedly discovered themselves. Unsurprisingly, such bold claims quickly drew attention across the security community, raising the question of whether Remus had truly found a Gmail zero-day, or whether it was all just a well-crafted sales pitch.\u003c/p\u003e"},{"bundle":"image","images":[{"sources":[{"srcset":"https://www.gendigital.com/blog/sites/default/files/styles/blogs_paragraph_image_large/public/2026-11/remus_blogpost_figure_01.jpg.webp?itok=XDiP_TOk 1x, https://www.gendigital.com/blog/sites/default/files/styles/blogs_paragraph_image_large_2x/public/2026-11/remus_blogpost_figure_01.jpg.webp?itok=y_CSEV8K 2x","media":"(min-width: 1020px)","type":"image/webp","width":1170,"height":458},{"srcset":"https://www.gendigital.com/blog/sites/default/files/styles/blogs_paragraph_image_medium/public/2026-11/remus_blogpost_figure_01.jpg.webp?itok=KmPwwXUs 1x, https://www.gendigital.com/blog/sites/default/files/styles/blogs_paragraph_image_medium_2x/public/2026-11/remus_blogpost_figure_01.jpg.webp?itok=Lzfkhjl3 2x","media":"(min-width: 720px)","type":"image/webp","width":890,"height":348},{"srcset":"https://www.gendigital.com/blog/sites/default/files/styles/blogs_paragraph_image_small/public/2026-11/remus_blogpost_figure_01.jpg.webp?itok=AVNKiByM 1x, https://www.gendigital.com/blog/sites/default/files/styles/blogs_paragraph_image_small_2x/public/2026-11/remus_blogpost_figure_01.jpg.webp?itok=eLCQS3BW 2x","media":"(min-width: 420px)","type":"image/webp","width":690,"height":270}],"caption":"Figure 1: The Remus developers promoting the new Gmail interception feature on an underground forum. ","url":"https://www.gendigital.com/blog/sites/default/files/styles/blogs_paragraph_image_small/public/2026-11/remus_blogpost_figure_01.jpg.webp?itok=AVNKiByM","link":""}]},{"bundle":"markdown","content":"\u003cp\u003eThe first to point out Remus' email interception feature outside underground forums was \u003ca href=\"https://x.com/g0njxa/status/2119470171802714453?a=O8UwVyAGD1w6c02qB5w\u0026amp;s=20\"\u003eg0njxa in a post on X\u003c/a\u003e, and a day later, Hudson Rock followed up with \u003ca href=\"https://www.hudsonrock.com/blog/remus-stealer-markets-persistent-gmail-access-hudson-rock-investigates\"\u003ea more in-depth investigation\u003c/a\u003e. As part of this investigation, they reached out to the Remus developers, who, however, revealed only that the feature works by interacting with the browser's memory, declining to disclose any further technical details to avoid \"quick fixes\", and, presumably, to protect what is arguably one of their product's main selling points at the moment.\u003c/p\u003e"},{"bundle":"image","images":[{"sources":[{"srcset":"https://www.gendigital.com/blog/sites/default/files/styles/blogs_paragraph_image_large/public/2026-11/remus_blogpost_figure_02.jpg.webp?itok=cneU-vmD 1x, https://www.gendigital.com/blog/sites/default/files/styles/blogs_paragraph_image_large_2x/public/2026-11/remus_blogpost_figure_02.jpg.webp?itok=w1Z7xSiR 2x","media":"(min-width: 1020px)","type":"image/webp","width":1170,"height":406},{"srcset":"https://www.gendigital.com/blog/sites/default/files/styles/blogs_paragraph_image_medium/public/2026-11/remus_blogpost_figure_02.jpg.webp?itok=OeCtWrQr 1x, https://www.gendigital.com/blog/sites/default/files/styles/blogs_paragraph_image_medium_2x/public/2026-11/remus_blogpost_figure_02.jpg.webp?itok=_KrpG0XW 2x","media":"(min-width: 720px)","type":"image/webp","width":890,"height":309},{"srcset":"https://www.gendigital.com/blog/sites/default/files/styles/blogs_paragraph_image_small/public/2026-11/remus_blogpost_figure_02.jpg.webp?itok=7nLwplJA 1x, https://www.gendigital.com/blog/sites/default/files/styles/blogs_paragraph_image_small_2x/public/2026-11/remus_blogpost_figure_02.jpg.webp?itok=qbW9lbrw 2x","media":"(min-width: 420px)","type":"image/webp","width":690,"height":239}],"caption":"Figure 2: The Remus developers’ description of the Gmail interception feature (source: Hudson Rock). ","url":"https://www.gendigital.com/blog/sites/default/files/styles/blogs_paragraph_image_small/public/2026-11/remus_blogpost_figure_02.jpg.webp?itok=7nLwplJA","link":""}]},{"bundle":"markdown","content":"\u003cp\u003eTo get the other side of the story, Hudson Rock also questioned a Remus customer who had publicly endorsed the feature. When asked, the customer echoed the developers' claims, replying that mailbox access persisted even after the victim closed the browser, reset their sessions, and changed their Google password.\u003c/p\u003e\n\u003cp\u003eAt this point, it is worth emphasizing that claims made by threat actors on underground forums might be far from reality, if not entirely made up, and the same goes for endorsements from their customers. Such claims should therefore always be independently verified. Hudson Rock thus rightly approached these statements with caution, concluding only that Remus most likely obtains reusable authorization from the victim's signed-in browser, while also openly acknowledging that their investigation included no technical analysis of the feature and that they could therefore neither confirm the existence of a Gmail zero-day vulnerability nor determine how the feature actually works.\u003c/p\u003e\n\u003cp\u003eRemus' bold claims have since been picked up by a number of security vendors and the media, yet the key questions remain unanswered. To the best of our knowledge, there is still no public technical analysis verifying whether Remus can really do what it promises, let alone explaining how it achieves it. In this blog post, we therefore set out to answer both, tracking down the code behind the feature and dissecting how it works under the hood.\u003c/p\u003e\n\u003ch2\u003eTechnical Analysis\u003c/h2\u003e\n\u003cp\u003eBefore diving into the technical details, it is worth noting that, based on the publicly available information, it was not clear where the interception functionality actually resides. The feature may have been implemented directly in the malware running on the victim's machine, but it could just as easily have been handled entirely on Remus' backend, in which case there might have been no code to analyze at all (especially considering that Remus presents the feature as a zero-day vulnerability and its developers refuse to share any technical details about it). That said, the developers behind Remus did admit that the feature works by interacting with the browser's memory, which was already a strong clue that at least some part of it might run directly on the victim's machine.\u003c/p\u003e\n\u003cp\u003eBesides the email interception feature, Remus has recently introduced several other features, such as a built-in clipper, a fake Ledger, and a reverse proxy. While we will not cover them in this blog post, there is one detail worth pointing out – all of these features are advertised as standalone modules (see Figure 3). The email interceptor, on the other hand, is notably not listed as one, suggesting that it might instead be implemented directly in the core stealer binary.\u003c/p\u003e"},{"bundle":"image","images":[{"sources":[{"srcset":"https://www.gendigital.com/blog/sites/default/files/styles/blogs_paragraph_image_large/public/2026-11/remus_blogpost_figure_03.jpg.webp?itok=OX21ghBX 1x, https://www.gendigital.com/blog/sites/default/files/styles/blogs_paragraph_image_large_2x/public/2026-11/remus_blogpost_figure_03.jpg.webp?itok=6EZ4p1Uw 2x","media":"(min-width: 1020px)","type":"image/webp","width":1170,"height":784},{"srcset":"https://www.gendigital.com/blog/sites/default/files/styles/blogs_paragraph_image_medium/public/2026-11/remus_blogpost_figure_03.jpg.webp?itok=d11eQ7_l 1x, https://www.gendigital.com/blog/sites/default/files/styles/blogs_paragraph_image_medium_2x/public/2026-11/remus_blogpost_figure_03.jpg.webp?itok=2hpZfxE7 2x","media":"(min-width: 720px)","type":"image/webp","width":890,"height":597},{"srcset":"https://www.gendigital.com/blog/sites/default/files/styles/blogs_paragraph_image_small/public/2026-11/remus_blogpost_figure_03.jpg.webp?itok=Ln9gOWPy 1x, https://www.gendigital.com/blog/sites/default/files/styles/blogs_paragraph_image_small_2x/public/2026-11/remus_blogpost_figure_03.jpg.webp?itok=RGuNVXDb 2x","media":"(min-width: 420px)","type":"image/webp","width":690,"height":463}],"caption":"Figure 3: Remus’ documentation of its features. After some additional hunting, however, we eventually managed to track down the code behind the feature and found that, contrary to what the Remus documentation suggests, it is implemented as a module after all.\u003c/p\u003e\n\u003ch3\u003eDynamic Configuration Retrieval\u003c/h3\u003e\n\u003cp\u003eRather than being embedded directly in the stealer binary, Remus' additional modules are delivered only at runtime as part of the stealer's dynamic configuration, retrieved from the C2 server. Besides the modules, this configuration also defines the stealer's collection rules, covering targeted browsers and their extensions, cryptocurrency wallets, applications, files, and registry values. As these rules can be customized by each operator, the scope of collected data may vary significantly from one operator to another, as well as across individual builds.\u003c/p\u003e\n\u003cp\u003eImportantly, this variability also applies to the stealer's modules, which is why the email interception module is only present in a subset of Remus builds. We suspect this is a consequence of Remus' tiered subscription model, with the module being reserved for the higher tiers or provided only to operators who specifically opt for the feature.\u003c/p\u003e\n\u003cp\u003eAs for the retrieval, the dynamic configuration is not delivered as a single blob, but rather split into several smaller parts, which Remus downloads from the C2 server one at a time. Each of them is encrypted separately using ChaCha20, with the 32-byte key and 8-byte nonce prepended to the ciphertext. Not at all. Based on our research, Remus only reuses a well-known technique documented as early as 2015 by \u003ca href=\"https://kaimi.io/2015/04/google-chrome-and-secure-preferences/\"\u003eKaimi\u003c/a\u003e, who described how Chrome calculates the HMAC values protecting its Secure Preferences file. The technique was later analyzed in greater detail by Pablo Picazo-Sanchez, Gerardo Schneider, and Andrei Sabelfeld in their 2020 paper \u003ca href=\"https://www.cse.chalmers.se/~andrei/cans20.pdf\"\u003e\u003cem\u003eHMAC and \"Secure Preferences\": Revisiting Chromium-based Browsers Security\u003c/em\u003e\u003c/a\u003e, and more recently, in 2025, revisited by Synacktiv in their research \u003ca href=\"https://www.synacktiv.com/en/publications/the-phantom-extension-backdooring-chrome-through-uncharted-pathways\"\u003e\u003cem\u003eThe Phantom Extension: Backdooring chrome through uncharted pathways\u003c/em\u003e\u003c/a\u003e, which additionally covers domain-joined machines and the \u003cstrong\u003eDeveloper Mode\u003c/strong\u003e protection mentioned above. For this reason, we will not elaborate on why Remus modifies these particular JSON entries or how the corresponding MAC values are recomputed, and instead refer curious readers to the aforementioned research.\u003c/p\u003e\n\u003cp\u003eAs for the extension itself, Remus disguises it as \u003cstrong\u003eGoogle Sheets\u003c/strong\u003e, borrowing the description word for word from the legitimate \u003cstrong\u003eGoogle Docs Offline\u003c/strong\u003e extension. Every request it makes relies on the victim's valid Gmail session, so once the sessions are revoked (or the victim simply signs out), the interception stops working as well. This is not a flaw, but simply how browser extensions are designed to work: once granted host permissions for a website, an extension can send requests to it with the user's existing cookies, just as the website itself would. After all, any malware capable of silently installing an extension could just as well steal the session cookies directly.\u003c/p\u003e\n\u003cp\u003eIn other words, rather than exploiting any flaw in Gmail, what Remus really abuses is the fact that victims who change their password or revoke all sessions will most likely not notice the malicious extension still running in their browser, which eventually regains access to their new emails as soon as they sign back in.\u003c/p\u003e\n\u003cp\u003eThat said, the absence of a zero-day does not make the approach any less dangerous in practice. As long as the extension remains installed, Remus can indeed intercept and exfiltrate new emails that arrive in any of the victim's signed-in Gmail accounts.\u003c/p\u003e\n\u003ch2\u003eIndicators of you need to know\u003c/strong\u003e\u003c/p\u003e\n\u003cp\u003e\u003cstrong\u003eThe risk:\u003c/strong\u003e CheckoutSwitch campaigns use fake parcel notices, prize offers and cloud-storage warnings to request small payments. Gen Threat Labs traced payment requests to unrelated merchants selling different products or services, from Weber barbecue grills to Oral-B toothbrushes. In one case, personal details entered on fake DHL pages also appeared in a retailer's customer account.\u003c/p\u003e\n\u003cp\u003e\u003cstrong\u003eThe scale:\u003c/strong\u003e Gen observes 1.2 million unique emails from these campaigns daily, reaching 200,000 to 300,000 customers each day within our protected customer base alone.\u003c/p\u003e\n\u003cp\u003e\u003cstrong\u003eOur advice:\u003c/strong\u003e Verify unexpected delivery notices, offers and account warnings through the company's official website or app before entering personal or card details. Even a small fee can expose you to unwanted charges and misuse of your information.\u003c/p\u003e\n\u003c/blockquote\u003e\n\u003cp\u003eOur investigation began with a German-language email promising a Weber Spirit grill. The message claimed to be a promotion from Hagebau, a well-known hardware retailer in Germany, and told the recipient they had already won their new grill.\u003c/p\u003e\n\u003cp\u003eThe offer fit the summer barbecue season and borrowed the credibility of two familiar brands. It also contradicted itself: elsewhere in the same message claiming the recipient had won one, the person was urged to hurry for a chance to win.\u003c/p\u003e\n\u003cp\u003eIt was the kind of spam offer we see regularly, and our protections were already blocking this type of activity. Recognizing the deception, however, told us little about the business behind the scam. We wanted to understand how these campaigns turned someone's interest in a free grill into money, and what happened to the information collected along the way.\u003c/p\u003e\n\u003cp\u003eWe began checking how widely the campaign was circulating and following its links. The grill offer connected to infrastructure carrying promotions under other brands, including offers for Oral-B dental kits. As we examined more messages, we found retail prizes, parcel delivery notifications and warnings that cloud accounts or personal files were about to disappear.\u003c/p\u003e\n\u003cp\u003eSome destinations complicated the picture. An antivirus-expiry warning redirected us to McAfee's official website, raising the possibility that deceptive messages were being used to generate sales for genuine commercial services. Identifying a fake email was only the beginning of understanding that relationship.\u003c/p\u003e\n\u003cp\u003eWe followed selected campaigns through to checkout and examined the payment attempts they generated. The merchant records allowed us to identify the scam businesses requesting the money and compare what they offered with what the emails had promised.\u003c/p\u003e\n\u003cp\u003eIn a campaign using DHL branding, that trail led us to a retailer's customer account containing the details we had entered during a supposed parcel-delivery process.\u003c/p\u003e\n\u003ch2\u003eA €9.99 delivery fee, and a shop we had never visited\u003c/h2\u003e\n\u003cp\u003eThe DHL-themed pages showed a parcel containing an Apple iPad Pro. They took us through delivery questions and asked for our contact details and address, followed by a €9.99 payment to release the shipment.\u003c/p\u003e"},{"bundle":"image","images":[{"sources":[{"srcset":"https://www.gendigital.com/blog/sites/default/files/styles/blogs_paragraph_image_large/public/2026-11/01a_Fake_DHL_Delivery_Notice.png.webp?itok=q2XVCBXm 1x, https://www.gendigital.com/blog/sites/default/files/styles/blogs_paragraph_image_large_2x/public/2026-11/01a_Fake_DHL_Delivery_Notice.png.webp?itok=uF19ix75 2x","media":"(min-width: 1020px)","type":"image/webp","width":509,"height":1077},{"srcset":"https://www.gendigital.com/blog/sites/default/files/styles/blogs_paragraph_image_medium/public/2026-11/01a_Fake_DHL_Delivery_Notice.png.webp?itok=nRoQ39VD 1x, https://www.gendigital.com/blog/sites/default/files/styles/blogs_paragraph_image_medium_2x/public/2026-11/01a_Fake_DHL_Delivery_Notice.png.webp?itok=ZGEYnALZ 2x","media":"(min-width: 720px)","type":"image/webp","width":509,"height":1077},{"srcset":"https://www.gendigital.com/blog/sites/default/files/styles/blogs_paragraph_image_small/public/2026-11/01a_Fake_DHL_Delivery_Notice.png.webp?itok=MDxYKxQe 1x, https://www.gendigital.com/blog/sites/default/files/styles/blogs_paragraph_image_small_2x/public/2026-11/01a_Fake_DHL_Delivery_Notice.png.webp?itok=PrirHVyV 2x","media":"(min-width: 420px)","type":"image/webp","width":509,"height":1077}],"caption":"Figure 1. The fake DHL pages show a parcel containing an iPad Pro and request delivery confirmation. The actual worldwide volume is much higher.\u003c/p\u003e\n\u003cp\u003eThe messages borrowed identities across several industries. Delivery lures used DHL, UPS, DPD and La Poste. Retail promotions and reward offers used names including Hagebau, Lidl, Walmart, Weber and Oral-B. Other messages impersonated healthcare services or used antivirus, streaming and cloud-account warnings. Whatever the brand, the narratives played on a few basic emotions: fear of losing something, such as personal files or a held parcel, and hope of gaining something, such as a prize or payout. These emotions drove both the click and the payment.\u003c/p\u003e\n\u003cp\u003eFor a separate analysis, we randomly selected about 33,000 emails per day over one month. The resulting dataset contained \u003cstrong\u003e1,090,839 unique emails\u003c/strong\u003e. Our classification showed that the traffic was overwhelmingly abusive: \u003cstrong\u003e59% was deceptive affiliate marketing, 25% was likely scams and 16% was suspicious affiliate marketing\u003c/strong\u003e. Most of the emails we analyzed fell into two categories: deceptive affiliate marketing and likely scams.\u003c/p\u003e\n\u003cp\u003eIn a separate analysis of the same dataset, we found that \u003cstrong\u003e98% of emails contained at least one Google Cloud Storage link\u003c/strong\u003e. These links redirected visitors through tracking and affiliate systems to an offer or checkout. Serving as the entry point to the affiliate network, they may help campaigns bypass spam and other email filters. Gen Security solutions already block this activity, even when the initial link uses a trusted cloud domain.\u003c/p\u003e\n\u003cp\u003eThe language distribution was heavily concentrated in English (51%) and French (35%), which together accounted for most of the analyzed emails. Limitless, Slots of Vegas, Lussurio\u003c/td\u003e\n\u003ctd\u003eClaims that a payout is waiting, leading to registration and deposit offers\u003c/td\u003e\n\u003c/tr\u003e\n\u003c/tbody\u003e\n\u003c/table\u003e\n\u003ch2\u003eWhat people and businesses stand to lose\u003c/h2\u003e\n\u003cp\u003eThe risks extend beyond the initial shipping fee. People can lose money on purchases they never intended to make, and some destinations introduce membership or recurring-payment terms. Providing card details through a deceptive process also exposes that information to possible theft or misuse.\u003c/p\u003e\n\u003cp\u003eThe Gearteria account showed a further consequence we could document directly: personal information collected during a fake delivery process appearing in an unrelated customer profile. That reuse creates risks of impersonation and identity fraud. A profile created without someone's knowledge also raises the risk that terms and conditions they have never seen could be accepted on their behalf, including automatic renewal of services and recurring charges. The profile itself was an observed finding. Acceptance of such terms, automatic renewal and subsequent identity theft were not established by our research.\u003c/p\u003e\n\u003cp\u003eThe companies being impersonated can suffer reputational damage and receive complaints about offers or charges they had nothing to do with.\u003c/p\u003e\n\u003cp\u003eMerchants receiving the referrals may also face consequences. A business that has not approved these methods could pay affiliate commissions for customers who were deceived about the purchase, then have to handle complaints, refunds and chargebacks.\u003c/p\u003e\n\u003cp\u003eConsumers can check the original claim before entering their details. A delivery notification can be verified through the courier's official website or app. A storage warning can be checked by opening the account independently. Unexpected promotions can be verified through the named company's own channels.\u003c/p\u003e\n\u003ch2\u003eThe business behind the spam\u003c/h2\u003e\n\u003cp\u003eWhat began with a grill offer led us to campaigns connecting familiar brands with unrelated commercial transactions. The payment records identified the merchants requesting the money, while affiliate tracking showed how referrals were credited. In the DHL case, we could also see where the personal information had gone.\u003c/p\u003e\n\u003cp\u003eSomeone agreeing to a small delivery fee can end up buying an unrelated product or service. A merchant paying affiliates in good faith may also be misled, funding promotions that deceive the customers it is paying to attract.\u003c/p\u003e\n\u003cp\u003eBusinesses need to examine the emails and pages generating those referrals and check what customers were promised before they arrived. In the campaigns we followed, someone arriving as a customer could still believe they were simply paying to receive a you need to know\u003c/strong\u003e\u0026nbsp;\u003c/h2\u003e\u003cp\u003eThe risk: AI chatbots can help you find products, download software or locate websites, but the links they recommend aren't always safe. Gen Threat Labs found examples of ChatGPT directing users to fake online stores, phishing websites and malicious downloads. Sometimes, even legitimate websites recommended by AI had been compromised by criminals.\u0026nbsp;\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eThe scale: Over 90 days, Gen security products blocked more than 260,000 threat-related events associated with AI chat services, including dangerous links and attacks hosted on AI platforms.\u0026nbsp;\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003e\u003cstrong\u003eOur advice:\u003c/strong\u003e Don't assume a website is trustworthy just because an AI chatbot recommended it. Double-check website addresses, be cautious when shopping at unfamiliar stores, and keep your security protection enabled.\u0026nbsp;\u003c/p\u003e"},{"bundle":"rich_text","content":"\u003cp\u003eFor years, cybercriminals have manipulated search results to put fraudulent stores, malicious downloads and phishing sites in front of people searching for legitimate services. The practice is generally known as SEO poisoning. As more people use AI assistants to find websites and products, similar risks are beginning to appear in chatbot answers.\u0026nbsp;\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003ePeople are getting used to asking chatbots for recommendations rather than searching for things themselves. And when an assistant gives you a helpful answer with a link to a website, it's easy to trust that recommendation without checking where it leads. Criminals can take advantage of that trust, particularly when they manage to get a fake store or malicious website included in an AI-generated answer.\u0026nbsp;\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eOur investigations uncovered several examples, including ChatGPT recommending a fake EcoFlow store, directing someone looking for legitimate software to a website distributing an infostealer, and linking to a respected journalism organization whose website had been compromised to redirect visitors to scams. Over the 90 days covered by our research, more than 121,000 Gen users were protected from threats associated with AI chat services.\u0026nbsp;\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eWe should also consider what happens as AI assistants start doing more things on our behalf. AI agents can increasingly navigate websites, download software and interact with online services, sometimes without requiring the user to approve every step. The attacks we've investigated mainly involve people clicking links recommended by chatbots, but these same malicious websites could also pose a risk to AI agents. As we give AI more freedom to act for us, checking the safety of the websites it interacts with will become increasingly important.\u0026nbsp;\u003c/p\u003e\u003ch2\u003e\u003cstrong\u003eFrom SEO poisoning to poisoned AI answers\u0026nbsp;\u003c/strong\u003e\u003c/h2\u003e\u003cp\u003eTraditional SEO poisoning attempts to influence where attacker-controlled content appears in search results. Now, a newer discipline called Answer Engine Optimization (AEO) is emerging, focused on making websites and their content more likely to appear in answers and recommendations from AI assistants. As AI agents become more widely used, businesses are also adapting their websites to make it easier for these systems to find, understand and use their content.\u0026nbsp;\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003e\u0026nbsp;Cybercriminals can exploit these same mechanisms to get malicious websites or manipulated information into AI-generated answers. This is where AI result poisoning comes in. MITRE ATT\u0026amp;CK added AI Result Poisoning as technique \u003ca href=\"https://attack.mitre.org/techniques/T1608/007/\" target=\"_blank\" rel=\"noreferrer noopener\"\u003eT1608.007\u003c/a\u003e in 2026, describing how attackers can manipulate publicly available\u0026nbsp; web content to influence what AI systems retrieve or recommend.\u0026nbsp;\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eHow do scam links end up in a chatbot's answer? We see four routes. First, the model can learn from poisoned data, so a bad recommendation is baked in before anyone asks. Second, the chatbot searches the web, finds content an attacker planted or a legitimate site the attacker has compromised, and passes it on as a citation or recommendation. Third, the model invents a plausible domain, such as a “.io” address for a well-known app, and a criminal registers it to carry out their scam. Fourth, the AI platform itself is only the host: attackers publish a lure in a shared conversation and send people there. From the outside we often cannot tell the first three apart. For the person who clicks, it makes no difference – all point to fraud.\u0026nbsp;\u003c/p\u003e"},{"bundle":"image","images":[{"sources":[{"srcset":"https://www.gendigital.com/blog/sites/default/files/styles/blogs_paragraph_image_large/public/2026-11/linkstoAI4ways%20%281%29_0.png.webp?itok=iixqL53r 1x, https://www.gendigital.com/blog/sites/default/files/styles/blogs_paragraph_image_large_2x/public/2026-11/linkstoAI4ways%20%281%29_0.png.webp?itok=FZWxUrVY 2x","media":"(min-width: 1020px)","type":"image/webp","width":1170,"height":658},{"srcset":"https://www.gendigital.com/blog/sites/default/files/styles/blogs_paragraph_image_medium/public/2026-11/linkstoAI4ways%20%281%29_0.png.webp?itok=JU9PBl4T 1x, https://www.gendigital.com/blog/sites/default/files/styles/blogs_paragraph_image_medium_2x/public/2026-11/linkstoAI4ways%20%281%29_0.png.webp?itok=OMMgcCy4 2x","media":"(min-width: 720px)","type":"image/webp","width":890,"height":501},{"srcset":"https://www.gendigital.com/blog/sites/default/files/styles/blogs_paragraph_image_small/public/2026-11/linkstoAI4ways%20%281%29_0.png.webp?itok=COHjO9mS 1x, https://www.gendigital.com/blog/sites/default/files/styles/blogs_paragraph_image_small_2x/public/2026-11/linkstoAI4ways%20%281%29_0.png.webp?itok=bhI6P7Rl 2x","media":"(min-width: 420px)","type":"image/webp","width":690,"height":388}],"caption":"Figure 1. Their websites can check a visitor's IP address, location, browser or operating system and decide what content to display. An automated crawler might see a perfectly legitimate page, while a regular user visiting the same URL gets redirected to a scam or a website distributing malware.\u0026nbsp;\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eThe same techniques can work against AI assistants. When ChatGPT retrieves information from a website, it may see legitimate content and include a link in its answer. But when someone clicks that link, the website can recognize the visitor and redirect them to a malicious destination. Attackers can also compromise a legitimate website after the AI assistant has accessed it.\u0026nbsp;\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eAI platforms have their own security measures, but attackers are constantly looking for ways around them, just as they have done with search engines for years. Our investigations show why users shouldn't automatically trust a website simply because an AI chatbot recommended it.\u0026nbsp;\u003c/p\u003e\u003ch2\u003e\u003cstrong\u003eHow people can reduce their risk\u0026nbsp;\u003c/strong\u003e\u003c/h2\u003e\u003cp\u003eWhen an assistant recommends a software download, check the developer's official domain before installing it. A familiar project name in the address bar is not enough, particularly when a lookalike site is distributing a modified installer. For unfamiliar stores, verify the business independently, including its contact details, policies, reputation and relationship to any brand it claims to represent.\u0026nbsp;\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eBe cautious with investment sites offering unusually attractive opportunities but little verifiable company information. Treat unexpected login screens, CAPTCHA-like instructions to run commands and AI-shared installation guides with similar skepticism. Keep browser and security protections enabled, including when the link originated from a trusted assistant rather than an email, message or search result.\u0026nbsp;\u003c/p\u003e\u003ch2\u003e\u003cstrong\u003eHelping people live Fearless in the AI era\u003c/strong\u003e\u0026nbsp;\u003c/h2\u003e\u003cp\u003eGen is already protecting people against many of the threats we've described in this investigation. Our web protection helps block malicious websites, phishing and scams, including those reached through AI chatbot recommendations. This is particularly important when dealing with compromised websites or malicious redirects, where the content a user encounters may be different from what the AI assistant saw earlier. We also use AI to help people identify scams, with solutions such as Norton Genie Scam Detector.\u0026nbsp;\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eAs AI assistants become more capable and start performing tasks on our behalf, we need to extend that protection beyond traditional browsing. Cybercriminals will keep finding new ways to abuse these technologies, and our job is to anticipate those threats and protect people from them.\u0026nbsp;\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eThat's how we help have found mobile subscription services sending payment authorization messages in a format that lets phones and browsers offer the code for autofill. The message explains the charge, including how often it repeats, but someone can accept the suggested code without opening it. In the iPhone example we tested, the suggestion displayed an amount while leaving out the fact that it would be charged every week.\u0026nbsp;\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eWe use the name BillBait for misleading subscription journeys that use SMS autofill to let people enter a payment authorization code without opening the message containing the subscription terms.\u0026nbsp;\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eAt Gen Threat Labs, we have been examining subscription journeys in Poland, Switzerland, Denmark and Canada. Our investigation looks at the use of these autofill-formatted messages in services charged through a mobile operator, and how that changes the information people encounter when making a purchase.\u0026nbsp;\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eMost of us are used to receiving codes by SMS. We enter them to sign into accounts, confirm transactions and prove that a phone number belongs to us. Having the phone retrieve the code saves us from switching between the message and the website, so accepting the suggestion can become as routine as entering the code ourselves.\u0026nbsp;\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eFor a subscription charged through a mobile operator, however, the message also explains the financial commitment associated with that code. Someone expecting a routine phone number verification may be authorizing a recurring payment.\u0026nbsp;\u0026nbsp;\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003ePaying through a mobile account has been around for years. Premium SMS services have been used to buy content and pay for services. Although the billing arrangements differ, some subscription journeys involve entering a phone number on a website and \u003ca href=\"https://archiwum.uke.gov.pl/aktualnosci/biezace-informacje/komunikaty/wakacje-co-musimy-wiedziec-o-czym-pamietac%2C6049.html\" target=\"_blank\" rel=\"noreferrer noopener\"\u003econfirming it with a code received by SMS\u003c/a\u003e.\u0026nbsp;\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eThe messages we examined use a format that associates the code with a website. Alongside the text intended for the customer, they include a machine-readable line containing the domain and the code. In the captured Polish message, that pair is:\u0026nbsp;\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003e\u003ccode\u003e@[REDACTED].com #2976\u0026nbsp;\u003c/code\u003e\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eWith the appropriate website and browser support, this allows the code to be offered for autofill. The domain helps the software determine where the code belongs, while the person can enter it without opening the message and rewriting it across.\u0026nbsp;\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eGoogle’s \u003ca href=\"https://web.dev/articles/sms-otp-form\" target=\"_blank\" rel=\"noreferrer noopener\"\u003eguidance on SMS codes\u003c/a\u003e describes banks and card issuers using them as an additional security check during a payment. In the subscription journeys we examined, the code itself is the only authorization and verification to start recurring charges on the user’s mobile account. Someone who believes they are verifying their phone number may therefore be completing a step that authorizes a paid subscription. The SMS explains what the code authorizes, including the price and billing frequency, but autofill allows the person to enter it without opening that message. A code intended to confirm a financial commitment can then be handled as casually as a routine login code.\u0026nbsp;\u003c/p\u003e"},{"bundle":"image","images":[{"sources":[{"srcset":"https://www.gendigital.com/blog/sites/default/files/styles/blogs_paragraph_image_large/public/2026-10/Screenshot%202026-10-21%20120007-2.png.webp?itok=zAQsjNnV 1x, https://www.gendigital.com/blog/sites/default/files/styles/blogs_paragraph_image_large_2x/public/2026-10/Screenshot%202026-10-21%20120007-2.png.webp?itok=UFJljuZb 2x","media":"(min-width: 1020px)","type":"image/webp","width":889,"height":1109},{"srcset":"https://www.gendigital.com/blog/sites/default/files/styles/blogs_paragraph_image_medium/public/2026-10/Screenshot%202026-10-21%20120007-2.png.webp?itok=6-fiIQZM 1x, https://www.gendigital.com/blog/sites/default/files/styles/blogs_paragraph_image_medium_2x/public/2026-10/Screenshot%202026-10-21%20120007-2.png.webp?itok=GUFD4O9n 2x","media":"(min-width: 720px)","type":"image/webp","width":889,"height":1109},{"srcset":"https://www.gendigital.com/blog/sites/default/files/styles/blogs_paragraph_image_small/public/2026-10/Screenshot%202026-10-21%20120007-2.png.webp?itok=PzqhIEg7 1x, https://www.gendigital.com/blog/sites/default/files/styles/blogs_paragraph_image_small_2x/public/2026-10/Screenshot%202026-10-21%20120007-2.png.webp?itok=L10g45RW 2x","media":"(min-width: 420px)","type":"image/webp","width":690,"height":861}],"caption":"Figure 1. Together, they illustrate two parts of the problem we are examining: how a request is presented to the customer, and how a payment authorization code is offered by the phone.\u0026nbsp;\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eFor anyone encountering these requests, opening the complete SMS before submitting the code is a useful precaution. Check that the service name matches what you intended to buy and that the amount is charged at the frequency you expect. If the message describes a subscription you were not looking for, stop there, even if the website has presented the code as a routine check.\u0026nbsp;\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eIf you discover an unfamiliar charge on your mobile bill, contact your operator through its official app, website or customer service number. Ask who is collecting the payment, how to cancel it and how to dispute the charge. Keep the messages and screenshots, and verify the cancellation instructions before following them. Some services use a STOP message, but blocking the sender or marking it as SPAM does not help, the charges will continue.\u0026nbsp;\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003ePeople who do not use premium services can also ask their operator about blocking them, checking separately what controls apply to third-party billing. The available options depend on the operator and country.\u0026nbsp;\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eOn the iPhone we tested, Apple’s autofill suggestion showed 14.99 zł without saying that the charge would repeat every week. Chrome displayed the full SMS, weekly charge included, but inside a browser prompt that carried Google's authority and was designed to be approved rather than read. The page was hosted on claude.ai, which is Anthropic’s own domain. It had Claude’s branding and what looked like simple instructions to install the desktop application. The attackers even paid for Google ads to send people there.\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eAnthropic, of course, never published such instructions. The attackers used Claude’s features for sharing conversations and Artifacts, and through them they placed malicious content on the real website. The page did not offer any installer. Instead, it told visitors to copy a command and paste it into the Terminal on their Mac. Everybody who followed these steps installed Atomic macOS Stealer (AMOS) — a malware that is made to steal passwords, browser sessions, cryptocurrency wallets and other sensitive data.\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eOur analysis found one more unpleasant thing. The malware can also replace the legitimate Ledger and Trezor desktop applications with modified versions. Later these fake versions ask the user to type the wallet recovery phrase, and this gives the attackers everything they need to take the victim’s cryptocurrency.\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eAnd this wasn't limited to macOS. We identified another campaign using Claude's sharing features and Google Ads to target Windows users. The installation instructions were different, as was the malware being delivered, but the approach was essentially the same.\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eNeither campaign required compromising Anthropic's infrastructure or registering a convincing imitation of its domain. The attackers simply took advantage of features available to ordinary Claude users, turning legitimate content-sharing functionality into a way to distribute malware.\u003c/p\u003e\u003ch2\u003eWhen the scam lives on the real domain\u003c/h2\u003e\u003cp\u003eMost phishing and scam campaigns depend on small details that the visitor is supposed to not notice — a domain name with a typo, a strange top-level domain, a missing padlock.\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eThis campaign removes all of that. The fake “Download Claude” page is real user-generated content hosted on claude.ai, and it is served in two ways: as a Shared Chat (claude[.]ai/share/…) and as a public Artifact (claude[.]ai/artifact/…). The command starts a chain of scripts that are downloaded from the attacker’s server, but for the operating system it looks like you typed it.\u003c/strong\u003e\u003c/li\u003e\u003cli class=\"ck-list-marker-bold\" data-list-item-id=\"e659ccd39d70f4d47697b4f8d3d876653\"\u003e\u003cstrong\u003eThe command is scrambled on purpose. What you get on your clipboard is an obfuscated one-liner that hides the attacker’s address inside an encoded blob. So even a curious user sees only gibberish and not a suspicious URL.\u003c/strong\u003e\u003c/li\u003e\u003c/ul\u003e\u003cp\u003eThe most useful habit you can build is one: never paste into your Terminal a command that you did not write yourself. No legitimate application — not Claude and not anything else — needs you to run a cryptic one-liner to be installed.\u003c/p\u003e\u003ch2\u003e\u003cstrong\u003eThe theft begins\u003c/strong\u003e\u003c/h2\u003e\u003cp\u003eThe moment you paste the command into your Terminal and press Enter, a chain of several stages runs completely in the background:\u003c/p\u003e\u003cul type=\"disc\"\u003e\u003cli class=\"ck-list-marker-bold\" data-list-item-id=\"e5e30139718460616724f21dd812ee6cb\"\u003e\u003cstrong\u003eA loader script is downloaded from the attacker’s server and it unpacks a second script.\u003c/strong\u003e\u003c/li\u003e\u003cli class=\"ck-list-marker-bold\" data-list-item-id=\"eb8f50c6940661ee2f7a9486df6524399\"\u003e\u003cstrong\u003eThis second script downloads the main payload — an AppleScript-based infostealer from the Atomic macOS Stealer (AMOS) 420px)","type":"image/webp","width":690,"height":1035}],"caption":"The whole attack, step by step: from the fake claude.ai page to the stolen data and, later, the theft of the wallet the stealer can take anything, it needs Full Disk Access. If it does not have this access, it does not start to steal. Instead it shows a fake macOS “Full Disk Access required” message and quietly adds a line to the shell startup file, so the malware starts again the next time you open the Terminal. In this way it simply waits until it gets the access it needs. Once it has the access, the stealer shows a familiar dialog: a fake macOS “System Preferences” password prompt. It looks native and it does not go away until you type a password that really works. After you type it, the malware has your login password in cleartext, and it keeps it.\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eAfter this it harvests, fast and quietly: Passwords and session cookies from Chromium and Gecko based browsers along with Keychain keys it needs to decrypt saved logins, crypto wallets both browser-extension and desktop applications and also files that usually hold secrets, private or otherwise interesting data such as with .pdf, .docx, .key, .seed or .pem, .wallet, or .ovpn extensions.\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eAll the collected data is packed into a ZIP file and uploaded to the attacker’s server. After the upload the stealer deletes its temporary files, so it leaves less trace behind.\u003c/p\u003e\u003ch2\u003eFake wallet apps\u003c/h2\u003e\u003cp\u003eWhen the stealer sees that Ledger Live, Ledger Wallet, or Trezor Suite is installed, it downloads a trojanized copy of this application from the attacker and puts it over the real one. The modified application looks and works like a normal one.\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eThe trap activates later. The next time when you open your wallet, a convincing screen asks you to “restore” or “validate” your recovery phrase (for Trezor it even shows a fake progress animation: “Reading device info… Validating seed phrase… Almost there”). The words that you type go directly to a second attacker server — a different domain than the one which delivered the malware, and it is built only to collect seed phrases.\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eThose words are your money. Anybody who has them can empty every wallet that is derived from them, immediately and without any way back.\u0026nbsp;\u003c/p\u003e\u003ch2\u003eThe same trick comes to Windows\u003c/h2\u003e\u003cp\u003eWe also identified a separate campaign targeting Windows users. But the method does not really care about the operating system, and we found a second campaign on claude.ai that works in exactly the same way — only the victim and the final malware are different.\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eThe lure is again a page that lives on the real domain. This time it is an Artifact with the title “Install Claude on Windows”, and it is pushed in the same way: the link carries Google Ads parameters (gclid, gad_campaignid), so the attackers again paid to place it high in the search results. The page shows the Claude logo, a friendly “Three quick steps and you’re done”, and the same thin “Content is user-generated and unverified” banner on the top that almost nobody reads.\u003c/p\u003e"},{"bundle":"image","images":[{"sources":[{"srcset":"https://www.gendigital.com/blog/sites/default/files/styles/blogs_paragraph_image_large/public/2026-10/Screenshot%202026-10-23%20at%2014.13.02.png.webp?itok=F83IIL2Y 1x, https://www.gendigital.com/blog/sites/default/files/styles/blogs_paragraph_image_large_2x/public/2026-10/Screenshot%202026-10-23%20at%2014.13.02.png.webp?itok=Io37tZxs 2x","media":"(min-width: 1020px)","type":"image/webp","width":1170,"height":756},{"srcset":"https://www.gendigital.com/blog/sites/default/files/styles/blogs_paragraph_image_medium/public/2026-10/Screenshot%202026-10-23%20at%2014.13.02.png.webp?itok=HAkPCDtA 1x, https://www.gendigital.com/blog/sites/default/files/styles/blogs_paragraph_image_medium_2x/public/2026-10/Screenshot%202026-10-23%20at%2014.13.02.png.webp?itok=UFJIzmmf 2x","media":"(min-width: 720px)","type":"image/webp","width":890,"height":575},{"srcset":"https://www.gendigital.com/blog/sites/default/files/styles/blogs_paragraph_image_small/public/2026-10/Screenshot%202026-10-23%20at%2014.13.02.png.webp?itok=ARbJfQwq 1x, https://www.gendigital.com/blog/sites/default/files/styles/blogs_paragraph_image_small_2x/public/2026-10/Screenshot%202026-10-23%20at%2014.13.02.png.webp?itok=vyRXU_25 2x","media":"(min-width: 420px)","type":"image/webp","width":690,"height":446}],"caption":"The Windows lure, hosted as a claude.ai Artifact: “Install Claude on Windows”. Instead of a real installer it gives three keyboard steps — Win + R, Ctrl + V, Enter — and the same small “user-generated and unverified” banner on the top.","url":"https://www.gendigital.com/blog/sites/default/files/styles/blogs_paragraph_image_small/public/2026-10/Screenshot%202026-10-23%20at%2014.13.02.png.webp?itok=ARbJfQwq","link":""}]},{"bundle":"rich_text","content":"\u003cp\u003eWhat changes is the ClickFix itself. On the Mac the victim is sent to the Terminal. On Windows the page asks you to press Win + R (this opens the Run dialog), then Ctrl + V to paste, and Enter to run. No Terminal, no file to download — only three keys, and the attack starts.\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eWhat you paste is a PowerShell command, and it is malicious from the first character — there is no installer in it at all. To look harmless while it runs, it first prints a big “SYSTEM” banner and calm messages like “Challenge in progress, this won’t take long… 50% done… 70% done… Challenge completed.” For the victim it looks like a normal verification step.\u0026nbsp;\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eThat script reaches out to GitHub. The attackers put their next stage on a github.io page, so once more a trusted name carries the malware. The file downloaded looks like an ordinary script, but inside it carries the\u0026nbsp; .NET payload, packed and encrypted.\u0026nbsp;\u003c/p\u003e\u003ch2\u003eThe final payload\u003c/h2\u003e\u003cp\u003eThe final payload is a heavily obfuscated .NET executable.\u0026nbsp;\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eThe behavior however, is clear enough. The malware hides itself by injecting its code into a normal Windows process, so in the task list nothing looks wrong. It checks which antivirus is present, it takes screenshots of the desktop, and it reads the title of the window where the user is working. It also opens a connection to a server of the attacker and keeps it — which means the operator can send more commands later. This is the behavior of a remote-access tool, not of a simple program that steals once and goes away.\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eAnd it goes after cryptocurrency. The malware searches the infected computer for crypto wallets — desktop applications like Electrum, Exodus, Ethereum, Bitcoin and Ledger, and also wallet extensions inside the browser.\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eTwo campaigns, two operating systems, two different pieces of malware — but one and the same front door: a page on claude.ai that looks official, pushed by paid ads, asking the user to run a single command they did not write.\u003c/p\u003e\u003ch2\u003eWhy this goes beyond Claude\u003c/h2\u003e\u003cp\u003eAttackers have been abusing legitimate websites to distribute malware for years. What makes this case interesting is how easily Claude's sharing features could be used to create something that looked like an official software download page, without compromising Anthropic's infrastructure.\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eClaude is unlikely to be the last AI service targeted this way. As more platforms allow users to publish and share content, attackers have more opportunities to exploit the reputation of those services. The challenge for platform operators is finding ways to prevent that abuse without making legitimate sharing unnecessarily difficult.\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eFor users, the distinction is worth remembering. A genuine website address tells you where content is hosted, but it doesn't necessarily tell you who created it or whether you should trust it.\u003c/p\u003e\u003ch2\u003eHow to protect yourself\u003c/h2\u003e\u003cul type=\"disc\"\u003e\u003cli data-list-item-id=\"edfc88eb7579f843c82adc66243e061d1\"\u003eNever paste into Terminal (or PowerShell) a command that you did not write yourself. This one habit defeats the whole ClickFix family.\u0026nbsp;\u003c/li\u003e\u003cli data-list-item-id=\"ec967e55d9b60bb6691b024d2a95e2d15\"\u003eDownload software from the main site of the vendor — and type the address yourself or use a bookmark. The real download page of Claude is claude.com/download. Do not trust the search results, and be extra careful with sponsored ads.\u003c/li\u003e\u003cli data-list-item-id=\"e55f655cf2bb6e06bc90158a0d59c4af7\"\u003eTreat the “user-generated / unverified” labels as a stop sign. If a “download” or “install” page lives on a sharing or artifact URL and not on the own product site of the company, leave it.\u003c/li\u003e\u003cli data-list-item-id=\"e6e92b09b36cd54ff7d342e668284ddba\"\u003eNever type your wallet recovery phrase into software that asks for it unexpectedly. The seed entry happens on the hardware device. A “restore/validate your phrase” prompt inside the app is theft.\u003c/li\u003e\u003cli data-list-item-id=\"edccf837b687961a074f5261a4b687f87\"\u003eKeep a reputable security software turned on. It blocks the known command-and-control servers and detects this behaviour.\u003c/li\u003e\u003cli data-list-item-id=\"e4e8607ff0369cb902840b9d76a86cd36\"\u003eIf you think that you already ran something like this, expect a full compromise. From a different, clean device, change every important password (email, banking and crypto exchanges first), log out from all sessions, and turn on multi-factor authentication. For crypto, move the funds to a wallet that is created from a completely new recovery phrase, and reinstall the wallet applications only from the official vendor.\u003c/li\u003e\u003c/ul\u003e"}],"itemState":"published","timeToRead":"14","translations":[],"originalLanguagePost":{},"mediaType":{"video":false,"audio":false},"socialMediaMetaImage":"https://www.gendigital.com/blog/sites/default/files/styles/social_media_meta/public/2026-10/claude_hero.jpg?h=10cbc464\u0026itok=fobM0kkk","relatedPosts":[{"urlAlias":"/blog-post/remus-stealer-email-interception-analysis","title":"Exposing
